========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hello doctor. Thank you for taking the time to see me. You know, I was just telling my neighbor the other day—she’s a lovely woman, always checking in on me since I've gotten older and spend most of my days around the house—I was telling her that I just don't know what gets into me lately. I spend a lot of time in my kitchen, you see. It's a small kitchen, but it's where I make all my old recipes, the ones my mother taught me back before I had to leave high school to help out at home. 

Lately... well, I'll make a big pot of something warm, you know, comfort food. Maybe some heavy stew or macaroni, the kind of food that just makes you feel safe and settled. And I sit down at my little table to have a bowl, just a regular bowl, because at my age, seventy-eight, you really don't need to be eating like a farmhand anymore. But then... it's the strangest thing, doctor. What brings me in is that once I start eating those comfort foods, I finish my bowl and I tell myself, "Maria, that is enough. You are done now." I say it right out loud sometimes! But my hand... my hand still goes back to the pot on the stove. It's like I can’t make myself stop. I just stand there with the spoon, going back for another bite, and another, even though I know I shouldn't be doing it. I just can't seem to stop my own hand, and it's been bothering me so much that I finally thought I ought to come in and ask somebody about it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it’s funny you ask about how fast I eat, doctor, because my whole life... my whole life I have always been such a slow eater. My late husband, God rest his soul, he used to tease me terribly about it. He’d say that by the time I finished my supper, it was already time to start cooking breakfast! I always believed in taking my time, you know, chewing every bite and really savoring the recipes my mother passed down to me. There's a certain respect you should have for a good meal, I always thought. 

But lately... when this strange thing happens with the pot on the stove, it’s completely different. I don't even go back to my little table to sit down like a civilized person. I just stand there right by the kitchen counter, leaning against it, and I eat so fast. It's like I'm in some kind of a race, but there's nobody else even in the house to take it away from me! I'm just standing there eating at this terrible speed, and I'm hardly even tasting the food at all. It's just... before one bite is even chewed up, the next bite is already going into my mouth. I don't know why I'm rushing so much. I just stand there staring at the tile backsplash—my husband put those yellow tiles in for me back in the eighties, such a messy job that was—and my hand is just moving so fast, bringing the spoon up over and over before I can even taste what I'm eating. It's so unlike me, it really is, and it just makes me feel so out of sorts because I've never been one to rush my food like a hungry dog.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, doctor... yes. Yes, I do. It’s just... well, it’s a terrible feeling, to be perfectly honest with you. A terrible, heavy feeling right here in my chest. You see, cooking and food, they were always such beautiful things in my family. When I had to leave school in the tenth grade—which broke my mother’s heart, but we just didn't have the money and she needed help with the younger ones—she taught me that feeding people is how you show them you love them. Every time I made her recipes, I felt like I was keeping her memory alive, keeping my husband's memory alive, too. Food meant family. It meant caring for people. 

But after I stand there at the counter and eat like that, so fast and so much... I look down at the empty pot, and I just feel this awful wave of shame wash right over me. I feel so sad, doctor, I could just cry right then and there. I take this beautiful thing that used to mean love and comfort, and I turn it into this... this ugly secret. I feel so guilty that I can't even look at myself in the mirror above the sink. I just quickly wash the pot and put it away so nobody will know. I mean, I go to church every Sunday at St. Jude's—I've sat in the fourth pew on the left for forty years—and I talk to the ladies after mass, and all I can think is, "If they knew what I did in my kitchen, if they knew how I ate like a wild animal, they would be so disgusted with me." I have to hide it from everyone, and it just breaks my heart that I’ve let it come to this. I've turned something so good into something I'm so deeply ashamed of.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, doctor, that's exactly it. That's exactly what I meant when I first sat down here with you today. It's like... it's like I'm completely disconnected from my own body. I remember telling you earlier, I sit there, and I say right out loud, "Maria, that is enough. You are done now." I mean it when I say it, too! I truly do. But then... it's the most frightening feeling, because my brain is saying one thing, but my hand... my hand just picks up that spoon and goes right back to the pot. 

It reminds me of when my sister Rosa—she was the youngest, always getting into trouble—when she was little, she would reach for the cooling cookies on the counter, and my mother would smack her hand with a wooden spoon. Rosa knew she shouldn't, but she just couldn't resist. Only I'm not a little girl reaching for a cookie, I'm a seventy-eight-year-old woman, and there's nobody here to smack my hand! I'm the one telling myself to stop, and I just... I can't. It's like a compulsion, or a motor running that I can't find the switch to turn off. I try so hard, doctor, I grip the edge of the counter sometimes, trying to hold myself back, but the urge is just so strong. It pulls me right back in, and before I know it, I'm eating again, completely out of my own control. It's like I'm a passenger in my own kitchen, just watching myself do something I desperately want to stop doing.

My late husband, God rest his soul, he used to tease me terribly about it. He’d say that by the time I finished my supper, it was already time to start cooking breakfast! I always believed in taking my time, you know, chewing every bite and really savoring the recipes my mother passed down to me. There's a certain respect you should have for a good meal, I always thought. \n\nBut lately... when this strange thing happens with the pot on the stove, it’s completely different. I don't even go back to my little table to sit down like a civilized person. I just stand there right by the kitchen counter, leaning against it, and I eat so fast. It's like I'm in some kind of a race, but there's nobody else even in the house to take it away from me! I'm just standing there eating at this terrible speed, and I'm hardly even tasting the food at all. It's just... before one bite is even chewed up, the next bite is already going into my mouth. I don't know why I'm rushing so much. I just stand there staring at the tile backsplash—my husband put those yellow tiles in for me back in the eighties, such a messy job that was—and my hand is just moving so fast, bringing the spoon up over and over before I can even taste what I'm eating. It's so unlike me, it really is, and it just makes me feel so out of sorts because I've never been one to rush my food like a hungry dog.\n\nDoctor: After these times when you're eating so quickly and can't seem to stop, do you feel upset with yourself or guilty about how much you've eaten?\n\nPatient: Oh, doctor... yes. Yes, I do. It’s just... well, it’s a terrible feeling, to be perfectly honest with you. A terrible, heavy feeling right here in my chest. You see, cooking and food, they were always such beautiful things in my family. When I had to leave school in the tenth grade—which broke my mother’s heart, but we just didn't have the money and she needed help with the younger ones—she taught me that feeding people is how you show them you love them. Every time I made her recipes, I felt like I was keeping her memory alive, keeping my husband's memory alive, too. Food meant family. It meant caring for people. \n\nBut after I stand there at the counter and eat like that, so fast and so much... I look down at the empty pot, and I just feel this awful wave of shame wash right over me. I feel so sad, doctor, I could just cry right then and there. I take this beautiful thing that used to mean love and comfort, and I turn it into this... this ugly secret. I feel so guilty that I can't even look at myself in the mirror above the sink. I just quickly wash the pot and put it away so nobody will know. I mean, I go to church every Sunday at St. Jude's—I've sat in the fourth pew on the left for forty years—and I talk to the ladies after mass, and all I can think is, \"If they knew what I did in my kitchen, if they knew how I ate like a wild animal, they would be so disgusted with me.\" I have to hide it from everyone, and it just breaks my heart that I’ve let it come to this. I've turned something so good into something I'm so deeply ashamed of.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself going back to the pot and eating even after you've told yourself to stop, do you feel like you're not in control of yourself, or like you can't resist the urge to keep eating no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, doctor, that's exactly it. That's exactly what I meant when I first sat down here with you today. It's like... it's like I'm completely disconnected from my own body. I remember telling you earlier, I sit there, and I say right out loud, \"Maria, that is enough. You are done now.\" I mean it when I say it, too! I truly do. But then... it's the most frightening feeling, because my brain is saying one thing, but my hand... my hand just picks up that spoon and goes right back to the pot. \n\nIt reminds me of when my sister Rosa—she was the youngest, always getting into trouble—when she was little, she would reach for the cooling cookies on the counter, and my mother would smack her hand with a wooden spoon. Rosa knew she shouldn't, but she just couldn't resist. Only I'm not a little girl reaching for a cookie, I'm a seventy-eight-year-old woman, and there's nobody here to smack my hand! I'm the one telling myself to stop, and I just... I can't. It's like a compulsion, or a motor running that I can't find the switch to turn off. I try so hard, doctor, I grip the edge of the counter sometimes, trying to hold myself back, but the urge is just so strong. It pulls me right back in, and before I know it, I'm eating again, completely out of my own control. It's like I'm a passenger in my own kitchen, just watching myself do something I desperately want to stop doing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

The presentation is consistent with loss of control eating, pointing more strongly toward F50.2 (Binge eating disorder) and F50.9 (Eating disorder, unspecified). F94.9 (Selective mutism) has been ruled out as it is unrelated to the reported symptoms.",
  "is_final": false

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, doctor, exactly like I was just telling you. It's exactly that. You ask what goes through my mind, and... well, it's just such a jumble, to be honest. I'm standing there, looking down at the stove—let's say it's my mother's Sunday gravy, the one with the pork ribs that takes all afternoon to simmer, or maybe a big pot of macaroni and cheese. I start eating it because it's just so comforting, you know? The house gets so terribly quiet in the evenings since my husband passed, and those comfort foods... they just wrap around you like a warm blanket. 

But then I realize what I'm doing, and I tell myself, clear as a bell, 'Maria, that is enough.' I say it right out loud! But it's like my brain just completely disconnects from my arm. My hand still goes back to the pot, over and over, like I simply can’t make myself stop. I'm standing there thinking, 'Put the spoon down, Maria, go into the living room, turn on your television programs, call Mrs. Higgins next door, do something else,' but the thoughts just float away like smoke. I just watch my hand dip back into the food, and I feel completely helpless to it. 

You'd think by seventy-eight years old, a woman would have some self-discipline, wouldn't you? My husband always said I was the most stubborn, strong-willed woman he ever met. If I made up my mind about something, that was the end of the discussion. But in those moments at the counter, all that willpower is just gone. I know I'm doing it, I know I want to stop, but the hand just keeps moving. I just can't make it stop.

You know, I was just telling my neighbor the other day—she’s a lovely woman, always checking in on me since I've gotten older and spend most of my days around the house—I was telling her that I just don't know what gets into me lately. I spend a lot of time in my kitchen, you see. It's a small kitchen, but it's where I make all my old recipes, the ones my mother taught me back before I had to leave high school to help out at home. \n\nLately... well, I'll make a big pot of something warm, you know, comfort food. Maybe some heavy stew or macaroni, the kind of food that just makes you feel safe and settled. And I sit down at my little table to have a bowl, just a regular bowl, because at my age, seventy-eight, you really don't need to be eating like a farmhand anymore. But then... it's the strangest thing, doctor. What brings me in is that once I start eating those comfort foods, I finish my bowl and I tell myself, \"Maria, that is enough. You are done now.\" I say it right out loud sometimes! But my hand... my hand still goes back to the pot on the stove. It's like I can’t make myself stop. I just stand there with the spoon, going back for another bite, and another, even though I know I shouldn't be doing it. I just can't seem to stop my own hand, and it's been bothering me so much that I finally thought I ought to come in and ask somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about these times when you can't seem to stop eating — do you feel like you're eating much more rapidly than normal, or until you feel uncomfortably full?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s funny you ask about how fast I eat, doctor, because my whole life... my whole life I have always been such a slow eater. My late husband, God rest his soul, he used to tease me terribly about it. He’d say that by the time I finished my supper, it was already time to start cooking breakfast! I always believed in taking my time, you know, chewing every bite and really savoring the recipes my mother passed down to me. There's a certain respect you should have for a good meal, I always thought. \n\nBut lately... when this strange thing happens with the pot on the stove, it’s completely different. I don't even go back to my little table to sit down like a civilized person. I just stand there right by the kitchen counter, leaning against it, and I eat so fast. It's like I'm in some kind of a race, but there's nobody else even in the house to take it away from me! I'm just standing there eating at this terrible speed, and I'm hardly even tasting the food at all. It's just... before one bite is even chewed up, the next bite is already going into my mouth. I don't know why I'm rushing so much. I just stand there staring at the tile backsplash—my husband put those yellow tiles in for me back in the eighties, such a messy job that was—and my hand is just moving so fast, bringing the spoon up over and over before I can even taste what I'm eating. It's so unlike me, it really is, and it just makes me feel so out of sorts because I've never been one to rush my food like a hungry dog.\n\nDoctor: After these times when you're eating so quickly and can't seem to stop, do you feel upset with yourself or guilty about how much you've eaten?\n\nPatient: Oh, doctor... yes. Yes, I do. It’s just... well, it’s a terrible feeling, to be perfectly honest with you. A terrible, heavy feeling right here in my chest. You see, cooking and food, they were always such beautiful things in my family. When I had to leave school in the tenth grade—which broke my mother’s heart, but we just didn't have the money and she needed help with the younger ones—she taught me that feeding people is how you show them you love them. Every time I made her recipes, I felt like I was keeping her memory alive, keeping my husband's memory alive, too. Food meant family. It meant caring for people. \n\nBut after I stand there at the counter and eat like that, so fast and so much... I look down at the empty pot, and I just feel this awful wave of shame wash right over me. I feel so sad, doctor, I could just cry right then and there. I take this beautiful thing that used to mean love and comfort, and I turn it into this... this ugly secret. I feel so guilty that I can't even look at myself in the mirror above the sink. I just quickly wash the pot and put it away so nobody will know. I mean, I go to church every Sunday at St. Jude's—I've sat in the fourth pew on the left for forty years—and I talk to the ladies after mass, and all I can think is, \"If they knew what I did in my kitchen, if they knew how I ate like a wild animal, they would be so disgusted with me.\" I have to hide it from everyone, and it just breaks my heart that I’ve let it come to this. I've turned something so good into something I'm so deeply ashamed of.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself going back to the pot and eating even after you've told yourself to stop, do you feel like you're not in control of yourself, or like you can't resist the urge to keep eating no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, doctor, that's exactly it. That's exactly what I meant when I first sat down here with you today. It's like... it's like I'm completely disconnected from my own body. I remember telling you earlier, I sit there, and I say right out loud, \"Maria, that is enough. You are done now.\" I mean it when I say it, too! I truly do. But then... it's the most frightening feeling, because my brain is saying one thing, but my hand... my hand just picks up that spoon and goes right back to the pot. \n\nIt reminds me of when my sister Rosa—she was the youngest, always getting into trouble—when she was little, she would reach for the cooling cookies on the counter, and my mother would smack her hand with a wooden spoon. Rosa knew she shouldn't, but she just couldn't resist. Only I'm not a little girl reaching for a cookie, I'm a seventy-eight-year-old woman, and there's nobody here to smack my hand! I'm the one telling myself to stop, and I just... I can't. It's like a compulsion, or a motor running that I can't find the switch to turn off. I try so hard, doctor, I grip the edge of the counter sometimes, trying to hold myself back, but the urge is just so strong. It pulls me right back in, and before I know it, I'm eating again, completely out of my own control. It's like I'm a passenger in my own kitchen, just watching myself do something I desperately want to stop doing.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe exactly what goes through your mind during those moments when your hand goes back to the pot—do you feel like you're completely unable to stop yourself, no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: Yes, doctor, exactly like I was just telling you. It's exactly that. You ask what goes through my mind, and... well, it's just such a jumble, to be honest. I'm standing there, looking down at the stove—let's say it's my mother's Sunday gravy, the one with the pork ribs that takes all afternoon to simmer, or maybe a big pot of macaroni and cheese. I start eating it because it's just so comforting, you know? The house gets so terribly quiet in the evenings since my husband passed, and those comfort foods... they just wrap around you like a warm blanket. \n\nBut then I realize what I'm doing, and I tell myself, clear as a bell, 'Maria, that is enough.' I say it right out loud! But it's like my brain just completely disconnects from my arm. My hand still goes back to the pot, over and over, like I simply can’t make myself stop. I'm standing there thinking, 'Put the spoon down, Maria, go into the living room, turn on your television programs, call Mrs. Higgins next door, do something else,' but the thoughts just float away like smoke. I just watch my hand dip back into the food, and I feel completely helpless to it. \n\nYou'd think by seventy-eight years old, a woman would have some self-discipline, wouldn't you? My husband always said I was the most stubborn, strong-willed woman he ever met. If I made up my mind about something, that was the end of the discussion. I start eating it because it's just so comforting, you know? The house gets so terribly quiet in the evenings since my husband passed, and those comfort foods... they just wrap around you like a warm blanket. \n\nBut then I realize what I'm doing, and I tell myself, clear as a bell, 'Maria, that is enough.' I say it right out loud! But it's like my brain just completely disconnects from my arm. My hand still goes back to the pot, over and over, like I simply can’t make myself stop. I'm standing there thinking, 'Put the spoon down, Maria, go into the living room, turn on your television programs, call Mrs. Higgins next door, do something else,' but the thoughts just float away like smoke. I just watch my hand dip back into the food, and I feel completely helpless to it. \n\nYou'd think by seventy-eight years old, a woman would have some self-discipline, wouldn't you? My husband always said I was the most stubborn, strong-willed woman he ever met. If I made up my mind about something, that was the end of the discussion. But in those moments at the counter, all that willpower is just gone. I know I'm doing it, I know I want to stop, but the hand just keeps moving. I just can't make it stop.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring these episodes when you're eating rapidly and feel out of control, do you keep eating until you feel uncomfortably full?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, heavens, yes. Yes, I do. It’s... well, it’s just awful, doctor. I keep eating and eating, like I was telling you, and I don't stop until... well, until I just can't take another bite without feeling sick. By the time I finally put the spoon down and wash the pot, my stomach feels so tight, so terribly heavy and stretched out. It's not a pleasant fullness, not like after a nice Thanksgiving dinner where you just feel satisfied and maybe a little sleepy. No, this is... this is a painful kind of full. 

I usually have to go right into the living room—the one with the floral wallpaper my husband put up back in '92—and I just collapse into his old brown leather recliner. I have to unbutton my slacks, loosen my waistband completely, just to get a little relief, because I've gone so far past what felt comfortable. I just sit there, feeling so stuffed and heavy, and so angry with myself for letting it happen again. I mean, my mother used to say, 'Maria, your eyes are bigger than your stomach,' when I was a little girl and asked for a second helping of her lasagna. But this isn't my eyes being bigger than my stomach. This is me completely ignoring my stomach until it's practically screaming at me that it can't hold another drop. I just push past the point of being full, right into feeling completely uncomfortable and sick to my stomach. It's just a miserable, miserable feeling, doctor.

I always believed in taking my time, you know, chewing every bite and really savoring the recipes my mother passed down to me. I'm just standing there eating at this terrible speed, and I'm hardly even tasting the food at all. It's just... before one bite is even chewed up, the next bite is already going into my mouth. I don't know why I'm rushing so much. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I... I live alone now, you see. Ever since my Arthur passed away—it was five years ago this past November, the Tuesday right before Thanksgiving, actually—the house is just empty. So, yes, I'm always alone when this happens. Just me and the hum of the refrigerator. 

But I'll tell you this... if anyone were here, if my daughter Susan came over—she lives two towns away in Westbury, has three lovely children of her own, so busy, you know—but if she were to drop by unannounced while I was standing at the stove eating like that... oh, I would just die of embarrassment. I would absolutely hide it. I wouldn't let her see me. I'd probably drop the spoon right into the sink and pretend I was just stirring the pot or putting away leftovers. 

I remember once, Mrs. Higgins from next door—she's a lovely woman, a bit nosy, always peeking through her blinds—she knocked on the back door to bring over some of her zucchini bread while I was right in the middle of... well, right in the middle of eating a whole box of those little powdered donuts over the sink. I panicked, doctor. I shoved the box into the cabinet under the sink with the cleaning supplies, wiped my mouth on my sleeve like a child, and took three deep breaths before I unlocked the door. My heart was pounding out of my chest! I just couldn't bear the thought of her seeing me like that, out of control, stuffing my face. It's so shameful. A woman my age should have more dignity, shouldn't she? So, yes, I'm alone, but if I wasn't... I'd do everything in my power to make sure nobody ever saw me doing it.

I spend a lot of time in my kitchen, you see. It's a small kitchen, but it's where I make all my old recipes, the ones my mother taught me back before I had to leave high school to help out at home. \n\nLately... well, I'll make a big pot of something warm, you know, comfort food. Maybe some heavy stew or macaroni, the kind of food that just makes you feel safe and settled. But then... it's the strangest thing, doctor. What brings me in is that once I start eating those comfort foods, I finish my bowl and I tell myself, \"Maria, that is enough. You are done now.\" I say it right out loud sometimes! But my hand... my hand still goes back to the pot on the stove. It's like I can’t make myself stop. I just stand there with the spoon, going back for another bite, and another, even though I know I shouldn't be doing it. I just can't seem to stop my own hand, and it's been bothering me so much that I finally thought I ought to come in and ask somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about these times when you can't seem to stop eating — do you feel like you're eating much more rapidly than normal, or until you feel uncomfortably full?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s funny you ask about how fast I eat, doctor, because my whole life... my whole life I have always been such a slow eater. My late husband, God rest his soul, he used to tease me terribly about it. He’d say that by the time I finished my supper, it was already time to start cooking breakfast! I always believed in taking my time, you know, chewing every bite and really savoring the recipes my mother passed down to me. There's a certain respect you should have for a good meal, I always thought. \n\nBut lately... when this strange thing happens with the pot on the stove, it’s completely different. I don't even go back to my little table to sit down like a civilized person. I just stand there right by the kitchen counter, leaning against it, and I eat so fast. It's like I'm in some kind of a race, but there's nobody else even in the house to take it away from me! I'm just standing there eating at this terrible speed, and I'm hardly even tasting the food at all. It's just... before one bite is even chewed up, the next bite is already going into my mouth. I don't know why I'm rushing so much. I just stand there staring at the tile backsplash—my husband put those yellow tiles in for me back in the eighties, such a messy job that was—and my hand is just moving so fast, bringing the spoon up over and over before I can even taste what I'm eating. It's so unlike me, it really is, and it just makes me feel so out of sorts because I've never been one to rush my food like a hungry dog.\n\nDoctor: After these times when you're eating so quickly and can't seem to stop, do you feel upset with yourself or guilty about how much you've eaten?\n\nPatient: Oh, doctor... yes. When I had to leave school in the tenth grade—which broke my mother’s heart, but we just didn't have the money and she needed help with the younger ones—she taught me that feeding people is how you show them you love them. Every time I made her recipes, I felt like I was keeping her memory alive, keeping my husband's memory alive, too. Food meant family. It meant caring for people. \n\nBut after I stand there at the counter and eat like that, so fast and so much... I just quickly wash the pot and put it away so nobody will know. I've turned something so good into something I'm so deeply ashamed of.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself going back to the pot and eating even after you've told yourself to stop, do you feel like you're not in control of yourself, or like you can't resist the urge to keep eating no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, doctor, that's exactly it. Rosa knew she shouldn't, but she just couldn't resist. Only I'm not a little girl reaching for a cookie, I'm a seventy-eight-year-old woman, and there's nobody here to smack my hand! I'm the one telling myself to stop, and I just... I can't. It's like a compulsion, or a motor running that I can't find the switch to turn off. I try so hard, doctor, I grip the edge of the counter sometimes, trying to hold myself back, but the urge is just so strong. It pulls me right back in, and before I know it, I'm eating again, completely out of my own control. It's like I'm a passenger in my own kitchen, just watching myself do something I desperately want to stop doing.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe exactly what goes through your mind during those moments when your hand goes back to the pot—do you feel like you're completely unable to stop yourself, no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: Yes, doctor, exactly like I was just telling you. It's exactly that. You ask what goes through my mind, and... well, it's just such a jumble, to be honest. I'm standing there, looking down at the stove—let's say it's my mother's Sunday gravy, the one with the pork ribs that takes all afternoon to simmer, or maybe a big pot of macaroni and cheese. I start eating it because it's just so comforting, you know? The house gets so terribly quiet in the evenings since my husband passed, and those comfort foods... they just wrap around you like a warm blanket. \n\nBut then I realize what I'm doing, and I tell myself, clear as a bell, 'Maria, that is enough.' I say it right out loud! But it's like my brain just completely disconnects from my arm. My hand still goes back to the pot, over and over, like I simply can’t make myself stop. I'm standing there thinking, 'Put the spoon down, Maria, go into the living room, turn on your television programs, call Mrs. Higgins next door, do something else,' but the thoughts just float away like smoke. I just watch my hand dip back into the food, and I feel completely helpless to it. \n\nYou'd think by seventy-eight years old, a woman would have some self-discipline, wouldn't you? My husband always said I was the most stubborn, strong-willed woman he ever met. If I made up my mind about something, that was the end of the discussion. But in those moments at the counter, all that willpower is just gone. I know I'm doing it, I know I want to stop, but the hand just keeps moving. I just can't make it stop.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes when you're eating rapidly and feel out of control, do you keep eating until you feel uncomfortably full?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. Yes, I do. It’s... well, it’s just awful, doctor. I keep eating and eating, like I was telling you, and I don't stop until... well, until I just can't take another bite without feeling sick. By the time I finally put the spoon down and wash the pot, my stomach feels so tight, so terribly heavy and stretched out. It's not a pleasant fullness, not like after a nice Thanksgiving dinner where you just feel satisfied and maybe a little sleepy. No, this is... this is a painful kind of full. \n\nI usually have to go right into the living room—the one with the floral wallpaper my husband put up back in '92—and I just collapse into his old brown leather recliner. I have to unbutton my slacks, loosen my waistband completely, just to get a little relief, because I've gone so far past what felt comfortable. I just sit there, feeling so stuffed and heavy, and so angry with myself for letting it happen again. I mean, my mother used to say, 'Maria, your eyes are bigger than your stomach,' when I was a little girl and asked for a second helping of her lasagna. But this isn't my eyes being bigger than my stomach. This is me completely ignoring my stomach until it's practically screaming at me that it can't hold another drop. I just push past the point of being full, right into feeling completely uncomfortable and sick to my stomach. It's just a miserable, miserable feeling, doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat alone during these times when you feel out of control with your eating, or do you try to hide this behavior from others?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I live alone now, you see. Ever since my Arthur passed away—it was five years ago this past November, the Tuesday right before Thanksgiving, actually—the house is just empty. So, yes, I'm always alone when this happens. Just me and the hum of the refrigerator. \n\nBut I'll tell you this... if anyone were here, if my daughter Susan came over—she lives two towns away in Westbury, has three lovely children of her own, so busy, you know—but if she were to drop by unannounced while I was standing at the stove eating like that... oh, I would just die of embarrassment. I would absolutely hide it. I wouldn't let her see me. I'd probably drop the spoon right into the sink and pretend I was just stirring the pot or putting away leftovers. \n\nI remember once, Mrs. Higgins from next door—she's a lovely woman, a bit nosy, always peeking through her blinds—she knocked on the back door to bring over some of her zucchini bread while I was right in the middle of... well, right in the middle of eating a whole box of those little powdered donuts over the sink. I panicked, doctor. I shoved the box into the cabinet under the sink with the cleaning supplies, wiped my mouth on my sleeve like a child, and took three deep breaths before I unlocked the door. My heart was pounding out of my chest! I just couldn't bear the thought of her seeing me like that, out of control, stuffing my face. It's so shameful. A woman my age should have more dignity, shouldn't she? So, yes, I'm alone, but if I wasn't... I'd do everything in my power to make sure nobody ever saw me doing it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I always believed in taking my time, you know, chewing every bite and really savoring the recipes my mother passed down to me. Every time I made her recipes, I felt like I was keeping her memory alive, keeping my husband's memory alive, too. Food meant family. It meant caring for people. \n\nBut after I stand there at the counter and eat like that, so fast and so much... I take this beautiful thing that used to mean love and comfort, and I turn it into this... this ugly secret. I feel so guilty that I can't even look at myself in the mirror above the sink. I just quickly wash the pot and put it away so nobody will know. I mean, I go to church every Sunday at St. Jude's—I've sat in the fourth pew on the left for forty years—and I talk to the ladies after mass, and all I can think is, \"If they knew what I did in my kitchen, if they knew how I ate like a wild animal, they would be so disgusted with me.\" I have to hide it from everyone, and it just breaks my heart that I’ve let it come to this. I've turned something so good into something I'm so deeply ashamed of.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself going back to the pot and eating even after you've told yourself to stop, do you feel like you're not in control of yourself, or like you can't resist the urge to keep eating no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, doctor, that's exactly it. Yes, I do. It’s... well, it’s just awful, doctor. I keep eating and eating, like I was telling you, and I don't stop until... well, until I just can't take another bite without feeling sick. By the time I finally put the spoon down and wash the pot, my stomach feels so tight, so terribly heavy and stretched out. It's not a pleasant fullness, not like after a nice Thanksgiving dinner where you just feel satisfied and maybe a little sleepy. No, this is... this is a painful kind of full. \n\nI usually have to go right into the living room—the one with the floral wallpaper my husband put up back in '92—and I just collapse into his old brown leather recliner. I have to unbutton my slacks, loosen my waistband completely, just to get a little relief, because I've gone so far past what felt comfortable. I just sit there, feeling so stuffed and heavy, and so angry with myself for letting it happen again. I mean, my mother used to say, 'Maria, your eyes are bigger than your stomach,' when I was a little girl and asked for a second helping of her lasagna. But this isn't my eyes being bigger than my stomach. This is me completely ignoring my stomach until it's practically screaming at me that it can't hold another drop. I just push past the point of being full, right into feeling completely uncomfortable and sick to my stomach. It's just a miserable, miserable feeling, doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat alone during these times when you feel out of control with your eating, or do you try to hide this behavior from others?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I live alone now, you see. Ever since my Arthur passed away—it was five years ago this past November, the Tuesday right before Thanksgiving, actually—the house is just empty. So, yes, I'm always alone when this happens. Just me and the hum of the refrigerator. \n\nBut I'll tell you this... if anyone were here, if my daughter Susan came over—she lives two towns away in Westbury, has three lovely children of her own, so busy, you know—but if she were to drop by unannounced while I was standing at the stove eating like that... oh, I would just die of embarrassment. I would absolutely hide it. I wouldn't let her see me. I'd probably drop the spoon right into the sink and pretend I was just stirring the pot or putting away leftovers. \n\nI remember once, Mrs. Higgins from next door—she's a lovely woman, a bit nosy, always peeking through her blinds—she knocked on the back door to bring over some of her zucchini bread while I was right in the middle of... well, right in the middle of eating a whole box of those little powdered donuts over the sink. I panicked, doctor. I shoved the box into the cabinet under the sink with the cleaning supplies, wiped my mouth on my sleeve like a child, and took three deep breaths before I unlocked the door. My heart was pounding out of my chest! I just couldn't bear the thought of her seeing me like that, out of control, stuffing my face. It's so shameful. A woman my age should have more dignity, shouldn't she? So, yes, I'm alone, but if I wasn't... I'd do everything in my power to make sure nobody ever saw me doing it.\n\n[Last Question]\nAfter these episodes of eating rapidly and feeling uncomfortably full, do you ever make yourself sick, use laxatives, or exercise excessively to try to control your weight or get rid of the food you ate?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 78 / not_in_workforce / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, heavens no. No, no, I would never do anything like that. I mean, the very thought of making myself sick... it just goes against everything I was taught. My mother, rest her soul, she grew up during the Depression, you know, over in Brooklyn. She used to tell us stories about how they didn't even have enough potatoes to make a proper stew sometimes. So the idea of wasting food, of bringing it back up on purpose... she would have tanned my hide, even at my age! She always said food was a blessing from the Lord, and you don't throw away a blessing. 

And as for laxatives, well, I only take my little fiber supplement that Dr. Evans—my regular doctor, the one with the glasses who always wears those nice bow ties—he recommended it for my digestion. But I'd never take anything just to get rid of the food. It just seems so... violent, almost, to your poor body. 

And exercise? Oh, doctor, my knees just aren't what they used to be. I used to love walking down to the shops, Arthur and I would walk all the way to the park on Sundays, but now? With my arthritis acting up the way it does, especially when the weather turns damp like it has been this past week... well, just getting up from that brown recliner I told you about is a chore some days. So no, I don't go running around the block or doing jumping jacks or anything silly like that. I just sit there, feeling full and miserable and terribly guilty, and I just wait for the feeling to pass. It takes hours, sometimes, but I just sit with it. I don't try to undo it, because... well, I did the crime, so to speak, so I just suffer through the punishment. I just wish I could stop doing the crime in the first place, you know?

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello doctor. Thank you for taking the time to see me. You know, I was just telling my neighbor the other day—she’s a lovely woman, always checking in on me since I've gotten older and spend most of my days around the house—I was telling her that I just don't know what gets into me lately. I spend a lot of time in my kitchen, you see. It's a small kitchen, but it's where I make all my old recipes, the ones my mother taught me back before I had to leave high school to help out at home. \n\nLately... well, I'll make a big pot of something warm, you know, comfort food. Maybe some heavy stew or macaroni, the kind of food that just makes you feel safe and settled. And I sit down at my little table to have a bowl, just a regular bowl, because at my age, seventy-eight, you really don't need to be eating like a farmhand anymore. But then... it's the strangest thing, doctor. What brings me in is that once I start eating those comfort foods, I finish my bowl and I tell myself, \"Maria, that is enough. You are done now.\" I say it right out loud sometimes! But my hand... my hand still goes back to the pot on the stove. It's like I can’t make myself stop. I just stand there with the spoon, going back for another bite, and another, even though I know I shouldn't be doing it. I just can't seem to stop my own hand, and it's been bothering me so much that I finally thought I ought to come in and ask somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about these times when you can't seem to stop eating — do you feel like you're eating much more rapidly than normal, or until you feel uncomfortably full?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s funny you ask about how fast I eat, doctor, because my whole life... my whole life I have always been such a slow eater. My late husband, God rest his soul, he used to tease me terribly about it. He’d say that by the time I finished my supper, it was already time to start cooking breakfast! I always believed in taking my time, you know, chewing every bite and really savoring the recipes my mother passed down to me. There's a certain respect you should have for a good meal, I always thought. \n\nBut lately... when this strange thing happens with the pot on the stove, it’s completely different. I don't even go back to my little table to sit down like a civilized person. I just stand there right by the kitchen counter, leaning against it, and I eat so fast. It's like I'm in some kind of a race, but there's nobody else even in the house to take it away from me! I'm just standing there eating at this terrible speed, and I'm hardly even tasting the food at all. It's just... before one bite is even chewed up, the next bite is already going into my mouth. I don't know why I'm rushing so much. I just stand there staring at the tile backsplash—my husband put those yellow tiles in for me back in the eighties, such a messy job that was—and my hand is just moving so fast, bringing the spoon up over and over before I can even taste what I'm eating. It's so unlike me, it really is, and it just makes me feel so out of sorts because I've never been one to rush my food like a hungry dog.\n\nDoctor: After these times when you're eating so quickly and can't seem to stop, do you feel upset with yourself or guilty about how much you've eaten?\n\nPatient: Oh, doctor... yes. Yes, I do. It’s just... well, it’s a terrible feeling, to be perfectly honest with you. A terrible, heavy feeling right here in my chest. You see, cooking and food, they were always such beautiful things in my family. When I had to leave school in the tenth grade—which broke my mother’s heart, but we just didn't have the money and she needed help with the younger ones—she taught me that feeding people is how you show them you love them. Every time I made her recipes, I felt like I was keeping her memory alive, keeping my husband's memory alive, too. Food meant family. It meant caring for people. \n\nBut after I stand there at the counter and eat like that, so fast and so much... I look down at the empty pot, and I just feel this awful wave of shame wash right over me. I feel so sad, doctor, I could just cry right then and there. I take this beautiful thing that used to mean love and comfort, and I turn it into this... this ugly secret. I feel so guilty that I can't even look at myself in the mirror above the sink. I just quickly wash the pot and put it away so nobody will know. I mean, I go to church every Sunday at St. Jude's—I've sat in the fourth pew on the left for forty years—and I talk to the ladies after mass, and all I can think is, \"If they knew what I did in my kitchen, if they knew how I ate like a wild animal, they would be so disgusted with me.\" I have to hide it from everyone, and it just breaks my heart that I’ve let it come to this. I've turned something so good into something I'm so deeply ashamed of.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself going back to the pot and eating even after you've told yourself to stop, do you feel like you're not in control of yourself, or like you can't resist the urge to keep eating no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, doctor, that's exactly it. That's exactly what I meant when I first sat down here with you today. It's like... it's like I'm completely disconnected from my own body. I remember telling you earlier, I sit there, and I say right out loud, \"Maria, that is enough. You are done now.\" I mean it when I say it, too! I truly do. But then... it's the most frightening feeling, because my brain is saying one thing, but my hand... my hand just picks up that spoon and goes right back to the pot. \n\nIt reminds me of when my sister Rosa—she was the youngest, always getting into trouble—when she was little, she would reach for the cooling cookies on the counter, and my mother would smack her hand with a wooden spoon. Rosa knew she shouldn't, but she just couldn't resist. Only I'm not a little girl reaching for a cookie, I'm a seventy-eight-year-old woman, and there's nobody here to smack my hand! I'm the one telling myself to stop, and I just... I can't. It's like a compulsion, or a motor running that I can't find the switch to turn off. I try so hard, doctor, I grip the edge of the counter sometimes, trying to hold myself back, but the urge is just so strong. It pulls me right back in, and before I know it, I'm eating again, completely out of my own control. It's like I'm a passenger in my own kitchen, just watching myself do something I desperately want to stop doing.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe exactly what goes through your mind during those moments when your hand goes back to the pot—do you feel like you're completely unable to stop yourself, no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: Yes, doctor, exactly like I was just telling you. It's exactly that. You ask what goes through my mind, and... well, it's just such a jumble, to be honest. I'm standing there, looking down at the stove—let's say it's my mother's Sunday gravy, the one with the pork ribs that takes all afternoon to simmer, or maybe a big pot of macaroni and cheese. I start eating it because it's just so comforting, you know? The house gets so terribly quiet in the evenings since my husband passed, and those comfort foods... they just wrap around you like a warm blanket. \n\nBut then I realize what I'm doing, and I tell myself, clear as a bell, 'Maria, that is enough.' I say it right out loud! But it's like my brain just completely disconnects from my arm. My hand still goes back to the pot, over and over, like I simply can’t make myself stop. I'm standing there thinking, 'Put the spoon down, Maria, go into the living room, turn on your television programs, call Mrs. Higgins next door, do something else,' but the thoughts just float away like smoke. I just watch my hand dip back into the food, and I feel completely helpless to it. \n\nYou'd think by seventy-eight years old, a woman would have some self-discipline, wouldn't you? My husband always said I was the most stubborn, strong-willed woman he ever met. If I made up my mind about something, that was the end of the discussion. But in those moments at the counter, all that willpower is just gone. I know I'm doing it, I know I want to stop, but the hand just keeps moving. I just can't make it stop.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes when you're eating rapidly and feel out of control, do you keep eating until you feel uncomfortably full?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. Yes, I do. It’s... well, it’s just awful, doctor. I keep eating and eating, like I was telling you, and I don't stop until... well, until I just can't take another bite without feeling sick. By the time I finally put the spoon down and wash the pot, my stomach feels so tight, so terribly heavy and stretched out. It's not a pleasant fullness, not like after a nice Thanksgiving dinner where you just feel satisfied and maybe a little sleepy. No, this is... this is a painful kind of full. \n\nI usually have to go right into the living room—the one with the floral wallpaper my husband put up back in '92—and I just collapse into his old brown leather recliner. I have to unbutton my slacks, loosen my waistband completely, just to get a little relief, because I've gone so far past what felt comfortable. I just sit there, feeling so stuffed and heavy, and so angry with myself for letting it happen again. I mean, my mother used to say, 'Maria, your eyes are bigger than your stomach,' when I was a little girl and asked for a second helping of her lasagna. But this isn't my eyes being bigger than my stomach. This is me completely ignoring my stomach until it's practically screaming at me that it can't hold another drop. I just push past the point of being full, right into feeling completely uncomfortable and sick to my stomach. It's just a miserable, miserable feeling, doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat alone during these times when you feel out of control with your eating, or do you try to hide this behavior from others?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I live alone now, you see. Ever since my Arthur passed away—it was five years ago this past November, the Tuesday right before Thanksgiving, actually—the house is just empty. So, yes, I'm always alone when this happens. Just me and the hum of the refrigerator. \n\nBut I'll tell you this... if anyone were here, if my daughter Susan came over—she lives two towns away in Westbury, has three lovely children of her own, so busy, you know—but if she were to drop by unannounced while I was standing at the stove eating like that... oh, I would just die of embarrassment. I would absolutely hide it. I wouldn't let her see me. I'd probably drop the spoon right into the sink and pretend I was just stirring the pot or putting away leftovers. \n\nI remember once, Mrs. Higgins from next door—she's a lovely woman, a bit nosy, always peeking through her blinds—she knocked on the back door to bring over some of her zucchini bread while I was right in the middle of... well, right in the middle of eating a whole box of those little powdered donuts over the sink. I panicked, doctor. I shoved the box into the cabinet under the sink with the cleaning supplies, wiped my mouth on my sleeve like a child, and took three deep breaths before I unlocked the door. My heart was pounding out of my chest! I just couldn't bear the thought of her seeing me like that, out of control, stuffing my face. It's so shameful. A woman my age should have more dignity, shouldn't she? So, yes, I'm alone, but if I wasn't... I'd do everything in my power to make sure nobody ever saw me doing it.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes of eating rapidly and feeling uncomfortably full, do you ever make yourself sick, use laxatives, or exercise excessively to try to control your weight or get rid of the food you ate?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. No, no, I would never do anything like that. I mean, the very thought of making myself sick... it just goes against everything I was taught. My mother, rest her soul, she grew up during the Depression, you know, over in Brooklyn. She used to tell us stories about how they didn't even have enough potatoes to make a proper stew sometimes. So the idea of wasting food, of bringing it back up on purpose... she would have tanned my hide, even at my age! She always said food was a blessing from the Lord, and you don't throw away a blessing. \n\nAnd as for laxatives, well, I only take my little fiber supplement that Dr. Evans—my regular doctor, the one with the glasses who always wears those nice bow ties—he recommended it for my digestion. But I'd never take anything just to get rid of the food. It just seems so... violent, almost, to your poor body. \n\nAnd exercise? Oh, doctor, my knees just aren't what they used to be. I used to love walking down to the shops, Arthur and I would walk all the way to the park on Sundays, but now? With my arthritis acting up the way it does, especially when the weather turns damp like it has been this past week... well, just getting up from that brown recliner I told you about is a chore some days. So no, I don't go running around the block or doing jumping jacks or anything silly like that. I just sit there, feeling full and miserable and terribly guilty, and I just wait for the feeling to pass. It takes hours, sometimes, but I just sit with it. I don't try to undo it, because... well, I did the crime, so to speak, so I just suffer through the punishment. I just wish I could stop doing the crime in the first place, you know?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No, this is... this is a painful kind of full. \n\nI usually have to go right into the living room—the one with the floral wallpaper my husband put up back in '92—and I just collapse into his old brown leather recliner. I have to unbutton my slacks, loosen my waistband completely, just to get a little relief, because I've gone so far past what felt comfortable. I just sit there, feeling so stuffed and heavy, and so angry with myself for letting it happen again. I mean, my mother used to say, 'Maria, your eyes are bigger than your stomach,' when I was a little girl and asked for a second helping of her lasagna. But this isn't my eyes being bigger than my stomach. This is me completely ignoring my stomach until it's practically screaming at me that it can't hold another drop. I just push past the point of being full, right into feeling completely uncomfortable and sick to my stomach. It's just a miserable, miserable feeling, doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat alone during these times when you feel out of control with your eating, or do you try to hide this behavior from others?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I live alone now, you see. Ever since my Arthur passed away—it was five years ago this past November, the Tuesday right before Thanksgiving, actually—the house is just empty. So, yes, I'm always alone when this happens. Just me and the hum of the refrigerator. \n\nBut I'll tell you this... if anyone were here, if my daughter Susan came over—she lives two towns away in Westbury, has three lovely children of her own, so busy, you know—but if she were to drop by unannounced while I was standing at the stove eating like that... oh, I would just die of embarrassment. I would absolutely hide it. I wouldn't let her see me. I'd probably drop the spoon right into the sink and pretend I was just stirring the pot or putting away leftovers. \n\nI remember once, Mrs. Higgins from next door—she's a lovely woman, a bit nosy, always peeking through her blinds—she knocked on the back door to bring over some of her zucchini bread while I was right in the middle of... well, right in the middle of eating a whole box of those little powdered donuts over the sink. I panicked, doctor. I shoved the box into the cabinet under the sink with the cleaning supplies, wiped my mouth on my sleeve like a child, and took three deep breaths before I unlocked the door. My heart was pounding out of my chest! I just couldn't bear the thought of her seeing me like that, out of control, stuffing my face. It's so shameful. A woman my age should have more dignity, shouldn't she? So, yes, I'm alone, but if I wasn't... I'd do everything in my power to make sure nobody ever saw me doing it.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes of eating rapidly and feeling uncomfortably full, do you ever make yourself sick, use laxatives, or exercise excessively to try to control your weight or get rid of the food you ate?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. No, no, I would never do anything like that. I mean, the very thought of making myself sick... it just goes against everything I was taught. My mother, rest her soul, she grew up during the Depression, you know, over in Brooklyn. She used to tell us stories about how they didn't even have enough potatoes to make a proper stew sometimes. So the idea of wasting food, of bringing it back up on purpose... she would have tanned my hide, even at my age! She always said food was a blessing from the Lord, and you don't throw away a blessing. \n\nAnd as for laxatives, well, I only take my little fiber supplement that Dr. Evans—my regular doctor, the one with the glasses who always wears those nice bow ties—he recommended it for my digestion. But I'd never take anything just to get rid of the food. It just seems so... violent, almost, to your poor body. \n\nAnd exercise? Oh, doctor, my knees just aren't what they used to be. I used to love walking down to the shops, Arthur and I would walk all the way to the park on Sundays, but now? With my arthritis acting up the way it does, especially when the weather turns damp like it has been this past week... well, just getting up from that brown recliner I told you about is a chore some days. So no, I don't go running around the block or doing jumping jacks or anything silly like that. I just sit there, feeling full and miserable and terribly guilty, and I just wait for the feeling to pass. It takes hours, sometimes, but I just sit with it. I don't try to undo it, because... well, I did the crime, so to speak, so I just suffer through the punishment. I just wish I could stop doing the crime in the first place, you know?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
